### 2026-11-23 Beta system-D-Bus-present headless Chromium check

- MySQL preflight showed Beta microVM `a6914740b29a46219ff3a1134cf6f2d0` still `running/failed`, generation 14, with no queued/working Agent run. In the guest, `/run/dbus/system_bus_socket` existed; desktop/CDP listeners were closed before the test.
- Ran `diagnose-chromium-headless.sh` against the existing Beta guest with a fresh profile `/tmp/csa-chromium-headless-systembus-20261123/profile`, unique port `19346`, and a 60-second readiness budget. The script confirmed `system_bus_socket=present`; Chromium remained active during five probes, but no CDP listener appeared. Since no listener appeared, HTTP client comparisons did not run. No DSH task ran.
- The script stopped the exact diagnostic process group and returned `sequence.complete`; an independent follow-up confirmed port `19346` was closed. Seven Chromium child processes remained in `Z` state under the guest's non-reaping PID 1; no live diagnostic listener remained.
- This controlled check shows that a present system D-Bus is not sufficient to make Chromium CDP ready. Together with earlier no-system-bus failures, neither D-Bus absence nor presence alone explains the failure. The cause remains unresolved.
- The diagnostic script reported process-level exit code 0 despite `chrome.result result=timeout`; that obscured a failed readiness outcome. Source now exits 5 after cleanup when CDP never becomes ready, while preserving exit 4 for a leaked listener. The guest run above used the prior source and has not been repeated with the corrected exit status; `bash -n` and `git diff --check` are the local checks for the correction.
- Current acceptance remains blocked at Beta desktop/CDP readiness. Do not infer DSH failure or success from this browser-only check.

### 2026-11-23 Beta Chromium CDP listener accepts HTTP but does not respond

- In the Beta generation 14 headless Chromium diagnostic on loopback port `19349`, CDP's TCP listener appeared on probe attempt 4 at elapsed 100 seconds. Chromium and guest PID 1 shared network namespace `net:[4026531840]`; the listener was visible in the guest's normal socket table, ruling out the earlier network-namespace hypothesis for this run.
- A bounded `strace` attached to the Chromium process captured Chrome accepting the local IPv4 connection and reading the complete 91-byte `GET /json/version HTTP/1.1` request. Before curl timed out and closed the socket, the trace showed no response write on that connection. The default curl, `Connection: close`, Python urllib, and Node HTTP probes all timed out; HTTP/1.0 was explicitly rejected by Chromium. This narrows the failure to the CDP HTTP request handler or its initialization after accept; it does not identify the Chromium internal root cause.
- The script's earlier exit code 0 meant only that a TCP listener existed, not that CDP returned a response. The working-tree diagnostic now distinguishes `http-ready`, `websocket-only-ready`, and `listener-no-response`, and returns nonzero when both HTTP and the direct browser WebSocket probe fail. Shell syntax and whitespace checks passed, but this revised script and its WebSocket probe have not yet been run in the guest.
- The exact diagnostic process group was terminated and the script's post-cleanup socket check confirmed port `19349` closed. No DSH task was started. Existing guest PID 1 behavior left additional defunct Chromium children; no live diagnostic Chrome listener remained.
- Next useful discriminator is the bounded direct connection to Chromium's logged browser WebSocket URL with `Browser.getVersion`, followed by `Target.getTargets` if it responds. If that succeeds while HTTP discovery remains unresponsive, adapt discovery to the browser WebSocket protocol and re-verify Browser/Computer against a real page; until then neither the desktop nor full Agent UI flow is accepted.

### 2026-11-23 Beta direct browser WebSocket diagnostic

- With no queued or working Beta Agent run, a fresh isolated profile started headless Chromium on loopback port `19351`. The CDP listener appeared on probe 4 at elapsed 94 seconds; guest system D-Bus was present. Default HTTP/1.1 curl, `Connection: close`, Python urllib, and Node HTTP did not complete a response; HTTP/1.0 again received an empty reply.
- The direct browser WebSocket probe used the template's Node `v24.22.3` runtime and timed out after 12 seconds with exit 137, producing no client-side open or CDP result line. A concurrent `strace` attached to the Chromium main process with 35 threads; its 363-line trace contained no `accept()` call, WebSocket Upgrade path, or `/devtools/browser` request during that probe. It mostly captured eventfd activity and a D-Bus error write. This does not prove why the WebSocket request never reached Chrome: client-side syscall tracing and a raw HTTP Upgrade comparison are still needed.
- The diagnostic correctly returned exit 6 (`listener-no-response-cleaned`) after stopping its exact process group. Its cleanup record contains only defunct Chromium children under guest PID 1; the exit code confirms the script's final port-leak check did not find port `19351` listening. No task, template, snapshot, or tenant state was changed, and no DSH/Pi run was started.
- The live blocker remains guest Chromium/CDP readiness, after the TCP listener stage. Next isolate whether the WebSocket client attempts a loopback connect and whether a raw Upgrade request receives HTTP 101, while keeping that distinct from the previously confirmed HTTP request accepted by Chrome with no response write. Do not attribute this to DSH, DeepSeek, or D-Bus without new evidence.

### 2026-11-23 Beta DevTools Upgrade and Chromium main-thread sample

- On Beta port `19353`, a raw HTTP/1.1 WebSocket Upgrade to Chrome's browser DevTools endpoint reached Chromium: the handler thread accepted the loopback connection and read the full 245-byte request, but no HTTP response was written; curl produced no response headers and timed out. The strace summary counted `accepts=2`, `upgrade_requests=1`, `responses=0`. The traced Node WebSocket client made zero `connect()` calls before timeout, so its earlier timeout alone is not evidence that Chrome rejected a WebSocket.
- Repeated the no-HTTP-matrix probe on port `19354` and sampled `/proc` thread CPU for 4.214 seconds once the listener appeared. The Chromium process leader (TID 748, `comm=chromium`) accumulated 1.93 seconds CPU (45.8% of the sample), remained `R`, and reported `wchan=0`, `syscall=running`. `Chrome_IOThread` and `Chrome_DevTools` were instead waiting in `epoll`; the raw Upgrade was again accepted and read with `responses=0`. This is direct evidence of a CPU-active Chromium main thread while its DevTools I/O thread waits, but the sampled thread was not symbolized, so the exact spinning function remains unknown.
- Current Chromium upstream routes `/json` HTTP requests and WebSocket requests from its DevTools handler thread onto the UI thread, then posts responses back to the handler thread ([DevToolsHttpHandler.cc](https://chromium.googlesource.com/chromium/src/+/main/content/browser/devtools/devtools_http_handler.cc#487)). Combined with the accepted/read/no-write trace and CPU sample, a stalled or busy UI task runner is now the leading hypothesis; it is not yet a proven root cause. The co-occurring D-Bus log is not enough to claim D-Bus caused it.
- Both diagnostic groups were terminated; the script returned exit 6 after its post-cleanup check found no surviving listener. No DSH task, template build, or tenant state transition occurred. Next collect a short CPU stack sample from the hot Chromium main thread (if guest perf support permits) before changing production launch flags or the template.

### 2026-11-23 Beta GDB stack sample follow-up

- Repeated the isolated headless run on Beta port `19356` with GDB's init files and debuginfod disabled and a 20-second attach/sample bound. CDP's TCP listener appeared after 79 seconds, while the HTTP and WebSocket endpoints again failed to return a response.
- GDB wrote 34 `[New LWP ...]` thread-discovery lines, then timed out with exit 137 and no `info threads`, backtrace, or instruction output. This is more evidence that the current GDB attach path cannot yield a usable stack within the bound; it does not establish whether GDB stalls while attaching, stopping all Chromium threads, or enumerating them. Do not interpret it as a Chromium function stack or root-cause proof.
- The diagnostic returned exit 6 (`listener-no-response-cleaned`) and the exact Chromium group was terminated. The follow-up read-only guest check confirmed port `19355` closed and the prior group had no live members; remaining entries are defunct children under PID 1. The Beta root filesystem remained 4.7 GiB available. No DSH task or production template change occurred.
- Next: use a lower-intrusion sampling path that does not require GDB to stop every Chromium thread (for example, verify guest kernel perf permissions and collect a bounded `perf` sample if supported), or build Chromium with symbols in an isolated diagnostic image. Keep the UI-thread stall as a hypothesis until a stack or a controlled launch-configuration comparison identifies the cause.

### 2026-11-23 Chromium session D-Bus environment discrepancy

- Comparing the real desktop launcher with the isolated headless diagnostic found an environment mismatch: `csa-desktop-session.sh` starts a D-Bus session before Chromium, while `diagnose-chromium-headless.sh` explicitly removed `DBUS_SESSION_BUS_ADDRESS`. Earlier headless reproductions therefore did not include the same session-bus setup as the desktop browser.
- The port `19356` Chromium log also contained repeated D-Bus address parse/connection errors. This makes session-bus setup a plausible factor, but the log correlation alone does not prove it caused the missing CDP responses.
- Added an opt-in `CSA_CHROMIUM_DBUS_SESSION=1` diagnostic mode using `dbus-run-session`, leaving the default diagnostic behavior unchanged. The next controlled run uses a new port and compares CDP HTTP/WebSocket behavior with a session bus before any production launcher change.

### 2026-11-23 bounded perf-top follow-up

- Re-ran the isolated Beta headless Chromium diagnostic on loopback port `19359` with a D-Bus session, HTTP and WebSocket response probes disabled, a 160-second CDP-listener budget and the CPU sampler enabled. CDP's TCP listener appeared on attempt 4 after about 81 seconds, visible through the Chromium process network namespace. This run confirms listener startup only; it does not re-measure HTTP or WebSocket responsiveness.
- Installed guest `linux-perf` had previously passed a `perf stat -e cpu-clock:u -- sleep 1` capability canary. However, `perf top --stdio -e cpu-clock:u -F 49 -d 1 -p 3528 --call-graph fp` produced no symbol sample: it exited on its 12-second timeout after reporting `Reading /proc/3528/task/3528/maps time out`. This means the profiler could not read Chromium's process mappings within its default bound; it does not identify the hot function or prove perf-event denial. Earlier `perf record` attempts also failed to finalize usable data, so do not repeat that attach/finalization path unchanged.
- Chromium logged `DevTools listening`. Because both response probes were disabled, the then-current classifier incorrectly labeled this unmeasured run `listener-no-response` and returned exit 6. A separate guest inspection confirmed port `19359` had no listener and the exact process group had no live members. The guest PID 1 still retained this and earlier Chromium children as zombies; those are defunct processes, not live browsers. No DSH task, template change, tenant state transition, or host Docker change occurred.
- The diagnostic now returns `listener-probe-incomplete` / exit 7 when a listener is found but one or both readiness probes were skipped. Earlier runs on ports `19349` and `19353` remain the direct evidence of HTTP and Upgrade requests accepted with no response. Keep the UI-thread-stall theory unproven until a symbolized sample or controlled configuration comparison succeeds.
- The follow-up read-only inventory confirmed `perf top` supports `--proc-map-timeout <n>`. Guest cgroup limits are 2 CPUs and 2 GiB; `memory.current` was 1,707,859,968 bytes (about 1.59 GiB), with `anon` about 175 MiB, `file` about 1.10 GiB and `kernel` about 327 MiB. `memory.events` still showed `oom=0` and `oom_kill=0`, so these figures do not establish OOM as the Chromium cause. Of 533 processes, 518 were zombies (191 Chromium); only 15 were live and none was Chromium. PID 1 is not reaping defunct children, which is a guest process-lifecycle issue and can distort further diagnostics.
- The same inventory surfaced two live leftovers from earlier profiling (`perf report` reading the invalid SIGINT sample and a `perf record -h` pager). Their exact process groups contained only `perf`, shell and pager processes; both groups were terminated and verified to have no live members. PID 1 retained the exited pager children as zombies. A future perf-help probe must set `PAGER=cat` and `PERF_PAGER=cat` to avoid leaving a pager behind.
- Do not launch another Chromium instance in this Beta guest until the process cleanup strategy is improved or the diagnostic is moved to a clean clone. A next profiling attempt, if still useful, should first verify the `--proc-map-timeout` units, use a bounded longer map-read timeout and ensure the parent reaps its children. No symbolized stack was obtained in this turn.

### 2026-11-23 clean-template clone Chromium and session-D-Bus checks

- First clean clone: build run `1225ceb8-6872-434d-bb1a-265cdea364b2`, temporary sandbox `851959d2fb064f35a26e9c2ce8baf076`, seed snapshot `snap-4029ae11937541db8609a8b6`. The restored desktop was stopped by exact component PID before the isolated Xvfb/direct-ELF check. UID 1000 could write the actual home, desktop home, and diagnostic profile. The guest logged `DevTools listening` on port `19222` after roughly 92 seconds, but later curl probes received no HTTP response. D-Bus logs included a missing system-bus socket and an invalid session address; these are observations, not a proven cause. The helper still used 36 fixed attempts, and the outer Cube PTY hit its 210-second deadline before the helper produced its final classification. The build `finally` path deleted the clone and did not create a snapshot.
- Second clean clone: build run `675ea53e-6f14-47c7-9272-cecdffea10f7`, temporary sandbox `ea28555874874955af13e3905cfaf26d`, same seed snapshot. A new D-Bus session was configured and XFCE reported ready. Direct ELF Chromium launched on port `19223`; four HTTP checks at elapsed 6, 18, 34, and 66 seconds timed out, and the final bounded wait ended at elapsed 136 seconds with `status=http-pending` and no listener reported by the helper. The system-bus socket was absent. Cleanup stopped the diagnostic display and D-Bus session, removed diagnostic profiles, and deleted the temporary sandbox; the run created no snapshot and started no DSH task.
- Together, the clean-clone checks show that a configured session D-Bus plus ready XFCE was not sufficient for CDP readiness, consistent with the earlier Beta run where a system-bus socket was present. D-Bus setup remains unproven as the root cause. Direct HTTP acceptance-without-response in earlier Beta runs remains the narrowest confirmed failure stage; no Chromium internal stack has yet been obtained.
- The first clone exposed a diagnostic-budget mismatch: a fixed probe count can outlive the enclosing guest-command deadline when guest commands are slow. `diagnose-chromium.sh` now uses a nominal 120-second wall-clock CDP deadline (overridable with `CSA_CHROMIUM_CDP_WAIT_SECONDS`, capped at 150 seconds), bounds package-owner lookup at 5 seconds, logs every probe, samples the process list in one batched command at 45-second intervals, and checks the actual guest TCP listener. A subsequent guest run validated that the helper completes, while the CDP loop's last in-flight probe and sample can extend the observed result beyond the nominal deadline; see the next section.

### 2026-11-23 clean-clone CDP listener and Chromium main-thread sample

- Ran build `41eaba41-4530-4433-a5c6-d0235ec6e5a1` against seed snapshot `snap-4029ae11937541db8609a8b6`, temporary sandbox `d5b5297740fb4a4eac7cd0c846d74036`. The helper's package-owner lookup hit its new 5-second timeout and logged exit `124`; remaining preflight and profile write probes completed. The updated guest command completed with exit `0` in `173,607 ms`, below the enclosing 210-second PTY limit. Build `finally` deleted the clone; a fresh `Sandbox.listV2` confirmed it was absent. No snapshot or DSH task was created.
- The helper independently read `/proc/net/tcp{,6}` on each attempt. Port `19222` was `absent` at the first probe and remained absent through the final timeout at guest elapsed `138s`; `/usr/lib/chromium/chromium` never logged `DevTools listening`, and no `/json/version` request returned HTTP 200. The final cleanup check also confirmed the listener was absent and no browser process matched the diagnostic profile. The nominal 120-second loop exceeded by 18 seconds because an in-flight guest probe and process sample finished after the loop deadline; the overall command still completed under its PTY limit.
- Process samples show ongoing Chromium startup work, not a socket-forwarding failure. The browser leader was runnable (`R`), `wchan=0`, `/proc/<pid>/syscall=running`, and CPU accumulated from about 53% at 5 seconds to about 52% at 100 seconds. It grew from one thread and about 6 MiB RSS at 5 seconds, to 14 threads/about 96 MiB at 52 seconds, then 24 threads/about 162 MiB at 100 seconds. At the last sample, the zygote and network-service child had also accumulated CPU. These samples narrow the failure to browser startup before the CDP listener; they do not identify the Chrome code path or root cause. Repeated D-Bus diagnostics remained in Chrome stderr but are not causal proof.
- The updated probe and cleanup behavior passed this guest run. Next compare the exact existing desktop flag set with a minimal Chromium startup on another disposable clone, preserving the same snapshot and profile ownership checks. Keep the root cause open until the minimal control or a usable stack identifies it.

### 2026-11-23 startup trace format and flush follow-up

- Build `d538691c-b30e-4cc1-b5fc-de7a132e440b` used disposable clone `db7affa1584446158550f14790fc930b`, timed out on `/json/version` at guest elapsed 261 seconds, and completed in 358.9 seconds. Cleanup deleted the clone and created no snapshot. Chromium logged completion of the trace and produced a 17,948-byte file; the first parser assumed UTF-8 JSON and failed. Chromium's tracing switches document `proto` as the default output format, so that parser assumption was wrong. The format supports JSON when controller-owned. [Chromium tracing switches](https://chromium.googlesource.com/chromium/src/%2B/a0e9c1e80c1bd2ab242a969fd460af4a07cae15e/components/tracing/common/tracing_switches.cc)

- Build `7aa239df-eb97-4193-98d4-e2062992cf3a` explicitly set `--trace-startup-owner=controller` and `--trace-startup-format=json`, waited up to five seconds after the completion log, and recorded file state and header bytes. The request no-response symptom is reproducible; the built-in trace has not provided usable task events, and its empty output cause remains unknown.

The working invocation must include both the dedicated env path and the same approved seed snapshot ID.
- Do not infer a UI-thread root cause from these trace attempts. Next inspect why controller-owned JSON startup tracing creates an empty file in this guest, or collect a bounded user-space stack through another supported mechanism. Preserve the CDP handler/UI task-runner explanation as an unconfirmed hypothesis.

### 2026-11-23 direct overlapping probe receives HTTP 200

- Build `01abda1f-a5b4-4d6c-b5a8-eca24ec68a57` used disposable clone `8a85657d0b25484aaf26d3b1faa8b019`, the same seed snapshot, `--no-startup-window`, and the host-staged ARM64 strace package. Chromium opened listener `127.0.0.1:19222` at guest elapsed 138 seconds. The normal readiness probe timed out with `curl_exit_code=124`, `http_code=000`, and zero bytes received.
- Strace then attached to 33 Chromium threads and launched a separate overlapping request to the same `/json/version` URL. That request completed successfully: `exit_code=0`, HTTP `200`, 435 response bytes, total time 0.437 seconds. The handler trace shows TID `16233` accepted the connection, read the 91-byte GET request, wrote a 568-byte HTTP 200 response, and then observed client EOF. Therefore the DevTools HTTP endpoint can answer during this run; earlier traces showing no response are time- and request-specific, and do not support the absolute claim that the handler never responds.
- The overall helper still reported `status=timeout` at elapsed 194 seconds because its readiness loop had already spent its final attempt timing out before the successful overlapping diagnostic request. This narrows the immediate failure to intermittent/delayed readiness and/or the probe schedule; it does not prove why the first probe missed the response, nor confirm the exact readiness behavior expected by the template clone check.
- Chromium process and listener were absent after cleanup. The temporary clone was deleted, no snapshot was created, and no tenant data was changed. Total build duration was 281.691 seconds. Full log: `~/.local/share/csa-cube-node/build-logs/template-build-2026-11-23T13-58-04.390Z-01abda1f.jsonl`.
- Next change the disposable-clone verification to preserve and report the response body plus timing for repeated `/json/version` probes across startup, and treat a successful direct probe as endpoint evidence even if an earlier poll timed out. Then rerun the actual template clone check with no profiler/strace; do not claim the template is ready until that check succeeds reliably.

### 2026-11-24 DevTools overlap trace and trace-output parser gap

- Run `7470c9f7-ce9a-4178-818f-5896c0189f9d` cloned seed `snap-4029ae11937541db8609a8b6` into disposable builder `9f6e968e79df4d6b8408481eb054a6de`. The requested 210-second sample window correctly used an effective 270-second CDP wait. Chromium's listener first appeared at guest elapsed 168 seconds and was owned by main PID `16010`; the sampled listener queue was empty (`rx_queue=0`, `tx_queue=0`). Requests before and after listener appearance still timed out. During the overlapping request the main thread `16010` was `R` and `Chrome_DevTools` TID `16270` was sleeping in `epoll_wait`.
- `strace` attached to TID `16270`, but the diagnostic summary reported a 163-byte trace file and zero counted calls. The exporter only printed lines prefixed with the numeric TID, while this single-thread trace was timestamped; therefore the raw trace was omitted from the host log. This is a trace parser/output defect, not evidence that no `accept`, `read`, or `write` occurred. The overlap curl exited `124`, recorded no HTTP status and no response body; the result logger also emitted missing-file noise because curl had not created its output file. The script now prints the bounded raw trace independent of prefix, counts calls by syscall name, pre-creates the body file, and records listener/queue state with the request result. Shell syntax and whitespace checks pass after these changes.

### 2026-11-24 correlate accepted TCP request with the missing response

- Run `7c824587-ae28-4a1a-9a8a-887b054ccba5` used disposable builder `f65553cebebe4a92aeeebd6e09805f99` from the known seed. The root is now narrowed to Chromium's post-read DevTools HTTP handling or a callback it waits on; the trace does not yet identify why the handler does not produce the response. Earlier aggregate accept counts were not enough to make this claim; this run provides the exact peer/fd match.

### 2026-11-24 CubeSandbox ownership-fix comparison

- Upstream CubeSandbox PR [#608](https://github.com/TencentCloud/CubeSandbox/pull/608) documents the same symptom: rootfs image ownership squashed to root can make a non-root Chromium user's profile/Crashpad unwritable so CDP never binds. The fix preserves UID/GID when running `umoci` as root and on the Docker-export tar path. PR [#671](https://github.com/TencentCloud/CubeSandbox/pull/671) removes `WithNoSameOwner` from the native image export path for the same reason. Both PRs merged before the v0.8.0 release.
- Read-only inspection of the active local node reports `cubecli version v0.8.0` (`Revision: v1`); its lifecycle-manager, proxy, and egress images are tagged v0.8.0 and were built on 2026-11-12. `Template.get` reports the base template READY at v2, with cube-agent/cube-shim v0.8.0 and an 8 GiB writable layer. These are useful version signals, but neither exposes the exact CubeMaster/TemplateCenter source revision that built the seed snapshot, so they do not attest that PR #608/#671 are in that specific build.
- The inspected seed's desktop home and Chromium profile directories are owned by UID/GID 1000; the integrated bootstrap also runs `chown -R` on `/workspace` before launching Chromium. This lowers the likelihood of a simple profile-owner mismatch, but the seed inspection did not test writes to the integrated profile's `Default` and Crashpad directories. Next run the integrated disposable desktop probe against the same seed and verify the new failure capture's exact profile writeability and bus fields before changing Cube versions or permissions.

The package download had not completed at the latest capture, so desktop/CDP and profile diagnostics are still pending.

Changed the predicate to check executables only; if the D-Bus config alone is missing, the installer now updates apt metadata and reinstalls only `dbus`, `dbus-daemon`, and `dbus-x11`, with explicit start/fail/complete events. `bash -n` and `git diff --check` pass. This fix was made after builder creation, so runtime verification requires a later probe.

### 2026-11-24 CubeSandbox upstream desktop and browser examples

- CubeSandbox's examples index explicitly lists two supported patterns: headless Chromium controlled by Playwright/CDP in a MicroVM, and an Ubuntu GNOME desktop served over noVNC. The browser example uses the prebuilt `sandbox-browser` image, CDP port `9000`, and template probe path `/cdp/json/version`; it is intended for automation and does not provide the human-visible desktop takeover surface.
- The Ubuntu desktop Dockerfile is based on `ghcr.io/tencentcloud/cubesandbox-base:2026.16`. It installs a desktop image at build time, including GNOME, dummy Xorg, an Xvfb fallback, D-Bus session support, x11vnc, and noVNC. Its startup script launches GNOME under `dbus-run-session`, starts `systemd-logind`, then exposes noVNC. Its documented Cube template example uses a 16 GiB writable layer, 4 CPU, 8 GiB memory, and ports 6080 and 49983. Docker is used to build/debug the image; Cube runs the resulting image as a MicroVM.
- Upstream issue [#1505](https://github.com/TencentCloud/CubeSandbox/issues/1505) reports Chromium GUI lag through VNC in a sandbox even with an 8-core/8-GiB allocation. The discussion distinguishes headless Playwright/CDP from interactive noVNC, suspects nested virtualization, and reports higher page-fault overhead with PVM shadow paging than L1 EPT on an animated page. This is a performance warning relevant to the Mac → Lima Linux → Cube MicroVM path, not proof that nested virtualization caused this project's CDP failure.
- Our current probe differs from the upstream desktop path: it installs packages after VM start and uses XFCE + Xvfb. The targeted package-repair fix now avoids reinstalling Chromium, and this run confirms D-Bus/IP-route repair is small. A post-session probe later found a stale `/run/dbus/system_bus_socket` file that rejects `dbus-send`; because this was observed after session cleanup, it is not evidence that the bus was unhealthy during Chromium startup. Before another full run, readiness should require a bounded successful D-Bus method call, while the CDP failure remains independently open.

The Cube SDK call passed `timeout: NEVER_TIMEOUT` (`-1`); the local CubeMaster config also has `default_timeout_insec: -1` and `create_timeout_insec: 600` (the latter bounds creation, not guest runtime). Cubelet then received `TaskExit` with code 3 and reported `container abnormal exit with code 3`; CubeShim's kill/delete/shutdown cleanup records followed the task exit. The Cube API log contains the original create and get response, but no public delete request at the shutdown time. - The template builder now records the requested idle timeout and lifecycle fields (`state`, `startedAt`, `endAt`, `timeout`) from Cube's control-plane responses. Guest file and command retries also check the sandbox lifecycle, fail immediately on a 404 or non-running state, and stop after 120 seconds of continuous data-plane failure instead of retrying for up to an hour. This protects the next probe from another unbounded wait; it does not explain the guest shutdown. The change has not yet been exercised against a fresh builder.

A fresh builder needs an early lifecycle snapshot and an independent guest shutdown/journal capture; keep the desktop package selection and template base explicit. The official Ubuntu Desktop example remains the preferred image recipe once the runtime failure is understood: it preinstalls GUI packages at image-build time and runs the resulting image in a Cube MicroVM.

This rules out absent KVM access for the current Cube service path; it does not establish GUI performance or prove the latest MicroVM launch.

### 2026-11-24 correct desktop base exposed a missing lock-directory setup

- The configured `CUBE_TEMPLATE_ID` was `coke-super-agent-dsh-v1` (`tpl-9b3f9b87fa5d4638bed5fd29`), not the existing READY `coke-super-agent-desktop-base-v2` (`tpl-fd4ebe4e82aa4949afd01007`). A `CSA_DSH_TEMPLATE_USE_BASE_TEMPLATE=1` probe initially still used the configured DSH template, which restored the same stale session PIDs and failed lock release. The Cube template list confirmed the separate desktop-base template exists.
- A disposable probe against the actual desktop-base-v2 (`8a35015b-a361-46c6-a922-7dda461fabdc`, builder `4519e670c9264618b36221553d2792be`) failed in 11.6 seconds before package installation. Its launcher log showed there was no desktop marker, session PID, X server, or lock holder; the preflight then called `flock` on `/workspace/.desktop-home/.csa-desktop-session.lock` before creating `/workspace/.desktop-home`, producing `flock: cannot open lock file ... No such file or directory`. No XFCE or CDP code ran; the disposable builder was cleaned and no snapshot was created.
- The launcher now creates the desktop user's home directory and lock file with UID/GID 1000 ownership and mode 0600 before probing the lock. It rejects symlink/non-file lock paths. This fix is only statically checked so far; the next probe must use `CUBE_TEMPLATE_ID=tpl-fd4ebe4e82aa4949afd01007` explicitly and verify the lock preflight before judging XFCE startup.
- A separate attempt to build `coke-super-agent-desktop-base-v3` from the pinned public ARM64 `sandbox-code` image failed during Cube replica creation. TemplateCenter reused a 377,487,360-byte rootfs artifact and distributed it successfully; Cubelet created the network runtime, VMM started two vCPUs, then CubeShim `CreatePodSandbox` failed after 10,111 ms with `Receive event timeout after 10000ms` (CubeMaster RequestId `f9320c77-cec7-4082-8955-ff25feaa9df6`; TemplateCenter job `0e3e32dc-0024-41a3-a54f-a2971037b331`). The template is FAILED and the clone was destroyed. This matches the known 10-second Cube agent/event window, but the guest-side point that missed the window is not established; do not conflate it with the lock-file failure.
- Next comparison should use the CubeSandbox base-image workflow and keep one GUI Chromium profile shared by CDP and noVNC. Measure image/template build time, time to a successful `/json/version` response, noVNC first frame, and a human-visible browser action separately. A listening socket or `DevTools listening` banner alone is not CDP readiness.

### 2026-11-24 lifecycle log correlation and PID 1 inspection

- Read-only node log correlation for the two probes above confirms VMM `VmExit::Shutdown` at guest uptime `114012 ms` and `138742 ms`; Cubelet then reports `TaskExit exit_status=3`. Its `cube.master.instance.kill_reason=request` annotation appears with the later `DestroyCubeSandboxRequest` issued during probe cleanup, not before the VMM shutdown. The trigger therefore remains inside the guest/runtime path, not the cleanup request.
- A third no-apt run `a81ae553-2bdf-4579-89d0-b99b3739746b` (sandbox `457bd8d0ab65431688e37e92878e71bf`) collected PID 1 details before the same exit. It used `timeout=-1`, held a foreground PTY, and Cube reported `unknown` / `restartState=Failed` at host elapsed `136329 ms`. VMM logged `VmExit::Shutdown` at guest uptime `132713 ms`; Cubelet then reported exit status 3. PID 1 executable was `/usr/local/bin/python3.12`, argv[0] was `python`, with 14 total arguments. The guest image lacks `strace`, `ps`, `journalctl`, and systemd; `dmesg` exposed only boot messages. The full argv was intentionally not copied because it was not needed to identify the executable, and no shutdown syscall was captured.
- This is consistent across three direct probes, but it does not yet prove the Python init process itself requested poweroff. The official Cube base image uses `tini` plus `cube-entrypoint.sh`, which starts envd and holds the supplied user command in the foreground; the current `sandbox-code`-derived template instead restores a Python PID 1. A controlled lifecycle comparison from the official base is now the next discriminating check. See the [official base image entrypoint contract](https://github.com/TencentCloud/CubeSandbox/blob/v0.8.0/docs/guide/tutorials/bring-your-own-image.md#4-the-entrypoint-contract).
- Run log: `~/.local/share/csa-cube-node/build-logs/cube-lifecycle-probe-2026-11-24T04-00-42.364Z-a81ae553.jsonl`. The probe VM was cleaned after the shutdown was observed; no template snapshot or tenant VM/task changed.

### 2026-11-24 official base template hits nested-startup handshake deadline

- The first official-base attempt used `ghcr.io/tencentcloud/cubesandbox-base:2026.16` and failed its GHCR layer download after 28.4/35 MB with an HTTP/2 `PROTOCOL_ERROR`; TemplateCenter was a host systemd process with no proxy environment. A one-run SSH reverse forward to the already-running Mac HTTP proxy (`127.0.0.1:7890`) and a temporary `/run` systemd drop-in were scoped only to `cube-sandbox-cube-templatecenter.service`. The retry pinned manifest digest `sha256:42a9610ae11fd19788a32fb2f54a50e744e569fa1690ca308c1d4c6cdf834f9a`, pulled the image, built the rootfs, and distributed it successfully.
- Proxy retry IDs: app run `5051ef1d-c428-4a7a-a7b8-df8f6fe2f8f8`, TemplateCenter build `1421693b-b434-4735-ac2c-dcb2d3dd07a7`, temporary template `tpl-a4698178775b41749a6a3ff4`, CubeMaster RequestId `0dcd007c-c73d-4aa0-91a0-e85c6a511518`. TemplateCenter reached `CREATING_TEMPLATE`; CubeShim create started at `04:22:20.672Z` and failed at `04:22:30.783Z` with `Create sandbox failed:Receive event timeout after 10000ms`. The failed temporary template was deleted by the probe's cleanup. No desktop image was built and no tenant VM was touched.
- The matching CubeShim serial records show the guest kernel still at uptime `[0.800090]` at `04:22:30.766Z`, immediately before the create timeout. There is no later kernel/userspace boot or agent-ready record for this sandbox. This separates the failure from wrong desktop packages, noVNC, XFCE, Chrome, and the official image's user CMD: the guest did not reach those stages. The same official image passed pull/rootfs/distribution, so this run also rules out the earlier registry transport failure as the cause of template creation failure.
- CubeSandbox v0.8.0 source confirms the exact failure path: `CubeShim/shim/src/sandbox/sb.rs` `start_vm()` (lines 1052–1068) calls `wait_notify()` with a hard-coded 10,000,000,000 ns deadline while waiting for `VsockServerReady`; `CubeShim/shim/src/hypervisor/cube_hypervisor.rs` (lines 270–276) formats expiration as `Receive event timeout after {}ms`. The observed `Receive event timeout after 10000ms` therefore identifies the Shim's 10-second readiness wait as the direct failure mechanism, rather than merely resembling an upstream issue. This does not prove why the guest misses the deadline. CubeSandbox upstream issue [#94](https://github.com/TencentCloud/CubeSandbox/issues/94) documents the same wait under nested KVM, though its reporter later closed after a simpler VM setup reached readiness in about 2 seconds. The official [template-timeout FAQ #312](https://github.com/TencentCloud/CubeSandbox/issues/312) lists slow nested startup as a cause, describes widening timeouts as a short-term workaround, and recommends PVM to avoid nested KVM. Our Lima config is `vmType: vz`, `arch: aarch64`, `nestedVirtualization: true`; the kernel log confirms an unusually large wall-clock/guest-clock boot gap. Nested startup is the leading explanation for the guest missing the deadline, but this causal explanation still needs a bounded longer-deadline run.
- PVM is not an available drop-in for this ARM64 Mac node: Cube's [Quick Start](https://github.com/TencentCloud/CubeSandbox/blob/master/docs/guide/quickstart.md) says its PVM host kernel is x86_64-only and ARM64 needs a Linux machine with native KVM. The proposed direct Apple Silicon backend [#975](https://github.com/TencentCloud/CubeSandbox/issues/975) was later closed as not planned. Increasing CubeMaster `create_timeout_insec` would not change the Shim's separate 10-second deadline. The proposed experiment is to increase that one wait to a bounded 60 seconds, then create the official base and verify guest-agent/health readiness; neither the patch nor its success is verified. At the time of recording, two other sandbox VMs are Up, so do not replace CubeShim or restart Cube services until an explicit maintenance window is agreed. Do not proceed to a multi-gigabyte desktop image until that create path succeeds.
- Cleanup completed: removed `/run/systemd/system/cube-sandbox-cube-templatecenter.service.d/90-csa-http-proxy.conf`, reloaded systemd, restarted only TemplateCenter, and verified the service is active without proxy variables. The temporary SSH forward was stopped and remote port `17891` is no longer listening. No persistent Mac networking setting was changed.
- Logs: `~/.local/share/csa-cube-node/build-logs/official-base-lifecycle-2026-11-24T04-21-34.531Z-5051ef1d.jsonl` and `~/.local/share/csa-cube-node/build-logs/official-base-lifecycle-2026-11-24T04-09-06.489Z-5693ae93.jsonl`.

### 2026-11-24 official Chrome ARM64 desktop probe failure

- Probe run `ffa54d4d-e4c4-4685-8219-7bab5f2b5d3c` used the READY base template `tpl-8e3320b4a33143dfa68d0b1d`; disposable builder sandbox `ce65a1a8f2194b69b6225d2f93894330`. Apt indexes and XFCE/noVNC packages installed. Google Chrome package metadata was arm64 version `158.0.8127.88-1`; its 142,083,448-byte download completed in 23.2 seconds.
- Chrome package extraction took about 11 minutes. A host-side `cubecli exec -t -i` process-tree snapshot showed `dpkg-deb --fsys-tarfile` as the active grandchild at 93.4% CPU, with 8m10s CPU time after 8m45s elapsed. It later exited and apt advanced from 47% to 98% and processed libc, fontconfig, and desktop-file triggers. The old bootstrap heartbeat sampled only apt-get and its direct dpkg child, so it hid the CPU-bound dpkg-deb grandchild; the heartbeat now records the complete descendant tree and CPU time.
- Xvfb, x11vnc, websockify, XFCE, and the Chrome process all started. Chrome never opened port 9222; after 210 seconds all 25 CDP probes returned connection refused. Failure capture showed the Chrome process still alive with 24 threads and no CDP socket. `/etc/machine-id` was zero bytes; Chrome logged that it expected 32 characters. The captured `chrome.failure.profile` had `session_bus=` empty, and Chrome also logged `Could not parse server address: Unknown address type`. These are confirmed environment defects and the leading explanation for the current Chrome startup failure; neither alone is proven to be the complete cause.
- At failure capture, root had 6.4 GiB free and `memory.events` reported `oom=0`; the earlier package-extraction snapshot also had no OOM kill. Disk exhaustion and OOM are not supported as causes of this probe failure.
- Builder cleanup completed at `07:05:01Z` with `builderCleanup=deleted`, no snapshot, and `cleanupFailed=false`. The two original tenant sandboxes were not modified. Run log: `~/.local/share/csa-cube-node/build-logs/template-build-2026-11-24T06-41-03.467Z-ffa54d4d.jsonl`.
- Follow-up patch creates and records a machine ID per sandbox ID (rotating the ID and restarting inherited desktop processes on a newly cloned tenant VM), then starts Chrome under `dbus-run-session`. Static `bash -n` and `git diff --check` pass. This change is not runtime-verified yet; the next run should build the complete snapshot and report machine-id, Chrome D-Bus address, CDP response, and clone checks separately.

### 2026-11-24 machine-id/D-Bus retry and process identity gap

- Intermediate run `3e4bd35d-3efe-49f1-ae05-6a537e9b922e` (`fd29e3a267f24359a5477a2109d1f6dd`) failed before apt in 3.6 seconds because the launcher called `dbus-uuidgen` before D-Bus packages were installed. The launcher now generates the per-sandbox 32-hex machine ID from the kernel UUID source before apt; the later bootstrap validates/reuses it and ensures `/var/lib/dbus/machine-id` points to `/etc/machine-id`.
- Full retry `6b173400-c260-4cab-8a7d-a451d714745d` used base template `tpl-8e3320b4a33143dfa68d0b1d` and disposable builder `46201b19f3574aeb960767023faff4db`. The guest machine ID was 32 hex characters plus newline and the D-Bus machine-id symlink was present. APT fetched indexes in about 9 seconds; Google Chrome arm64 `158.0.8127.88-1` downloaded in 13.6 seconds, while its unpack remained CPU-bound at roughly 94% and took about 10 minutes before apt reached 98%.
- Xvfb, x11vnc, websockify, XFCE, and the Chrome launch wrapper started. The wrapper's 360-second CDP wait made 33 probes; all returned connection refused on `127.0.0.1:9222`. The system D-Bus socket existed and the Chromium profile tree was writable by UID 1000. The Chromium log file was empty. XFCE logged optional AT-SPI service errors, panel provider timeouts, and missing `/usr/bin/pm-is-supported`; no evidence yet ties those warnings to Chrome's missing listener.
- This failure capture exposed an instrumentation defect: the PID recorded as Chrome (`10234`) was `/usr/bin/dbus-run-session`, sleeping in `do_wait` with 364 KiB RSS and zero CPU-tick delta. The logger sampled only that wrapper, not its child process tree. The `session_bus=` field also reads the parent desktop shell environment, not the isolated environment passed to the Chrome child by `dbus-run-session`; treat it as inconclusive. Do not claim that Chrome itself was idle or that its session bus was absent from this sample.
- The build failed in `desktop.bootstrap`; cleanup deleted the builder successfully and no snapshot was created. The original tenant sandboxes were not modified. Run log: `~/.local/share/csa-cube-node/build-logs/template-build-2026-11-24T07-11-03.866Z-6b173400.jsonl`.
- Next diagnostic patch adds bounded process-descendant samples during CDP waits and at timeout, including executable paths for Chrome/D-Bus processes, and surfaces those events as structured build-log fields. Static `bash -n` and `git diff --check` pass after the patch; this improved capture still needs a full template-build run before the Chrome child startup cause can be concluded.

The next diagnostic therefore traces all threads of only the PID that owns listener `127.0.0.1:9222`, with a longer attach window and a request sent after attach coverage is complete. Chrome logged the banner and opened `127.0.0.1:9222` at watcher elapsed 148 seconds; `/json/version` again connected and timed out with zero bytes. System and Chrome session D-Bus probes succeeded.
- The listener-owning Chrome process had 30 guest tasks. `strace -f -p 442` reported 29 attached threads before its 75-second limit expired; the diagnostic correctly marked coverage partial and did not launch its explicit overlap curl. The trace still captured Chrome's `Chrome_DevTools` TID `1411` accepting several connections and reading complete 90-byte `/json/version` GETs. It showed no write on those accepted TCP descriptors before clients closed; only the listener thread performed the observed CDP socket calls. This sample does not cover the remaining process task or existing Chrome child processes, so it cannot rule out work being handed elsewhere.

Improve attach readiness by checking whether the specific `Chrome_DevTools` TID is traced, and include a small, redacted per-thread syscall summary. This rules out the suspected special workspace mount, but does not explain the Chrome handler stall. The probe sandbox was killed after collecting `findmnt`, `statfs`, and `df` output.
- Chromium's upstream [`DevToolsHttpHandler`](https://chromium.googlesource.com/chromium/src/+/main/content/browser/devtools/devtools_http_handler.cc) handles the TCP request on its server thread, then posts `/json/...` handling to `GetUIThreadTaskRunner`; `/json/version` response generation runs from `OnJsonRequest` on that UI task runner. Given the observed 90-byte request read by `Chrome_DevTools` and no response write, the leading hypothesis is that the browser UI thread is not servicing that posted task. This is an inference from upstream code and the guest trace, not yet a confirmed thread-level diagnosis.
- The next sample adds a CPU, `wchan`, syscall, and kernel-stack snapshot of the hottest Chrome threads at the moment 9222 first listens. This targets the suspected UI-thread handoff without another broad debugger attach.

### 2026-11-25 paired route probe narrows the Chrome CDP stall

- Added one bounded probe for an intentionally unmatched path to the existing disposable-template watcher. Chromium's HTTP server should answer that route directly with 404; `/json/version` is routed through the DevTools handler's UI-thread task path. The probe records status, connect/total duration, exit code and sanitized curl error.
- Run `90e7ed90-6f75-4f7b-a423-ba7855608df7` created disposable sandbox `22d1c82728264c7cab40b1fa8315be6c` from READY candidate `tpl-827a52ee603c40f3992e40dd`. System D-Bus and Chrome's private session D-Bus `ListNames` both returned exit 0; `/etc/machine-id` was 33 bytes. Chrome PID `454` eventually opened IPv4 listener `127.0.0.1:9222`.
- At watcher sample 5 / elapsed 135 seconds, `GET /__csa_diagnostic_unmatched` returned HTTP 404 in 1.066 seconds (connect 0.581 seconds). In the same sample, `/json/version` connected in 0.204 seconds, then timed out after 5.134 seconds with HTTP `000` and zero response bytes. IPv6 correctly refused because Chrome listened on IPv4 only. This proves that TCP accept and the direct unknown-route response work while the `/json` path does not return in this window.
- The Chromium [`DevToolsHttpHandler`](https://chromium.googlesource.com/chromium/src/+/main/content/browser/devtools/devtools_http_handler.cc#487) posts `/json` requests to the UI thread; unknown non-`/devtools/` paths receive `Send404` directly on the handler thread. `/json/version` then builds a small version object and posts its response back to the handler thread. The route comparison therefore narrows the stall to the UI-dispatched `/json` path. It does not yet prove whether `OnJsonRequest` fails to run, fails to return, or whether its response-post step fails.
- At the listener sample, Chrome's main thread remained runnable and used about 18.2% of one CPU during the 54-second thread-sampling interval; `Chrome_DevTools` was sleeping in `epoll_wait` at the captured instant. These snapshots do not provide user-space frames for the pending callback, so the exact Chromium call site and root cause remain unknown.
- The 180-second watcher hit the TypeScript runner's 360-second outer deadline while sampling threads; `cdp.watch.complete` was not emitted. Final guest-state capture succeeded, then the disposable sandbox cleanup completed. No snapshot was created, the candidate template remains unactivated, the two original tenant sandboxes were not used, and no application tenant mapping changed. Full log: `~/.local/share/csa-cube-node/build-logs/template-chrome-threads-2026-11-24T19-14-44-762Z-90e7ed90-6f75-4f7b-a423-ba7855608df7.jsonl`.
- Local registry transport remained healthy during this run: the Mac loopback registry and the Lima-forwarded endpoint returned HTTP 200. No image rebuild or registry push was needed. Next diagnostic should trace the UI-thread callback/response-post boundary during paired `/json/version` and unmatched requests, with a smaller bounded sample so the runner can always finish cleanup inside its deadline.

The trace file contained 67,965 bytes in Perfetto's protobuf format, but this collector attempted to parse it as Chrome JSON and failed. This is a trace-parser defect; the run yielded no event-level conclusions. Chromium documents `proto` as the default startup trace format, and Perfetto's official `trace_processor convert json` command supports converting protobuf traces to Chrome JSON. [Chromium tracing switches](https://chromium.googlesource.com/chromium/src/%2B/a0e9c1e80c1bd2ab242a969fd460af4a07cae15e/components/tracing/common/tracing_switches.cc), [Perfetto conversion docs](https://perfetto.dev/docs/quickstart/traceconv)

The diagnostic wrapper now converts protobuf to JSON through stdin/stdout and keeps the raw trace in memory, then logs only a compact redacted event summary. It also accepts `CSA_TEMPLATE_CHROME_CDP_TIMEOUT_SECONDS` so a targeted trace run can use a 260-second readiness limit rather than the default 360 seconds; the trace duration remains 240 seconds. TypeScript, shell syntax, and whitespace checks passed after these changes; no tests were run.

No template snapshot was created, the candidate remains unactivated, and no tenant sandbox/task was touched. The Mac-local registry image and template were not rebuilt or changed.
- The first live endpoint check found that the Lima reverse-forward at `127.0.0.1:15002` had stopped even though the Mac registry still returned HTTP 200. Running `scripts/cube/ensure-local-api-tunnel.sh` restored the Cube API/Proxy forwards and registry reverse-forward; it verified the host registry at `127.0.0.1:15001` and the Cube-side endpoint at `127.0.0.1:15002`. No Cube service was restarted.

The follow-up trace included `poll`, `ppoll`, `epoll_wait`, `epoll_pwait`, `select`, and `pselect6`; in the 15-second browser-main-thread window it recorded 4 calls total (2 opens and 2 reads), with **zero** wait-system calls. One lookup for `~/.config/user-dirs.dirs` returned `ENOENT`. Chrome was still running and port 9222 had no listener at the sample.
- These traces support that Chrome's browser main thread is executing in user space during the sampled startup stall, rather than repeatedly reading files or waiting in `poll`/`epoll`. They do not identify the Chromium function or establish that the missing `user-dirs.dirs` file is causal. The exact root cause remains unknown.
- The trace scripts now support an opt-in 15-second main-thread-only mode and report wait-call counts and the last bounded set of relevant calls. Shell syntax, `git diff --check`, and TypeScript checks passed after this edit; no test suite was run. No template activation, tenant VM mutation, or snapshot creation has occurred.
- Follow-up run `921b7ce5-f67b-4505-8e6d-1aa849fe92b8` created disposable sandbox `2252f318efd44259b347b4e85bdfd7f9` and cleanup completed. Staging GDB's shared libraries in the guest overlay fixed the earlier no-exec mapping error, but the 60-second GDB attach emitted only `[New LWP]` notifications and exited 137 without producing a stack. GDB's startup also reports `ModuleNotFoundError: No module named 'gdb'`; that may be a packaging issue and is not yet established as the attach timeout cause. The outer diagnostic deadline was exceeded during cleanup, but the disposable sandbox was removed successfully.
- The next GDB attempt should disable automatic shared-library symbol loading and Python auto-scripts, and reduce thread-event chatter before attach. If it still cannot produce a main-thread backtrace within a short bound, stop repeating GDB and use an alternate single-thread unwinder or profiler. Chrome's exact user-space function and the root cause remain unconfirmed.
- Run `3896c1f6-eac7-4ec7-8473-43b2083f3339` used disposable sandbox `7b5c6eb2b88e49419c5d9f398556aa12` and cleaned it successfully. Passing GDB's data directory fixed its version check (`GNU gdb 12.1`), but the guest's test CDP wait was only 90 seconds: GDB extraction and startup took 216 seconds, by which point the failed Chrome process had exited, so no attach was attempted. The next and final bounded GDB probe raises only the disposable guest's CDP wait to 420 seconds, keeping Chrome alive long enough for the stack attempt. Automatic Python/shared-library loading is disabled. If it still fails, stop GDB retries and retain the syscall evidence as the best current diagnosis.
- Final bounded GDB run `bbb2a677-72fb-4124-ab64-5e11da9d35a0` created disposable sandbox `b19ba599b1e24933acba03367389cc55` and cleanup completed. GDB attached far enough to leave Chrome PID `455` in Linux state `tl` with wait channel `ptrace_stop`, but the 60-second attach produced zero output and exited 137. Therefore this attempt froze the browser without providing a user-space backtrace. Chrome opened `127.0.0.1:9222` at about guest elapsed 351 seconds; the subsequent `/json/version` timeout occurred while GDB had stopped the process and is not independent CDP evidence. Earlier no-GDB runs already showed that the endpoint can accept connections and then return zero bytes. Stop repeating the GDB path. The best current evidence is a Chrome main thread that makes no file-read or poll/epoll syscalls during a bounded 15-second sample while consuming CPU; exact Chromium function and root cause remain unresolved.

The raw-file resize has not been performed.
- The repository startup path originally required exactly 64 GiB. It now accepts the existing 64-GiB disk and an 80-GiB disk; new disks default to 80 GiB, with `CSA_CUBE_DATA_DISK_SIZE=64GiB` available for an explicit 64-GiB creation. ### 2026-11-25 XFS growth restored scheduling; diagnostic file upload used DNS instead of CubeProxy

- The authorized restart and filesystem growth completed. `cube-xfs` is 80 GiB; `/data/cubelet` reports about 59 GiB used and 22 GiB free, and CubeOps reports `DataDiskUsagePer=73.5363%`, `Healthy=true`, `ReportedReady=true`, `SchedulingDisabled=false`. The outer Cube sparsebundle remains 90 GiB with about 12 GiB free. Cube API health returned 200, and the local CubeProxy/Registry tunnels were restored. Docker, MySQL, K3D and Registry remained running through the Cube node restart.
- The one authorized disposable 2-vCPU/2048-MiB sandbox was created from template `tpl-d92cea70efc040cfb32580f2` in 763 ms: run `a06b780b-056f-4c55-a318-46e3552d7b5c`, sandbox `4cafd62bc7824e3fbfe285f5a09901f3`. This confirms the data-disk scheduling watermark was the cause of the preceding `130597`; the post-resize create was successful. The guest-file stage then failed at elapsed 4.8 seconds with `fetch failed`, and the diagnostic successfully killed the sandbox at elapsed 5.288 seconds. Cube API, CubeShim and VMM logs recorded creation and shutdown; a subsequent inventory found no remaining diagnostic VM. No image, template, backup or tenant data was deleted or replaced.
- The diagnostic's `sandbox.create.start` event did not include proxy settings. Inspection found that this standalone script constructed SDK `Config` with only the API endpoint and timeout; it therefore left `proxyNodeIp` unset. For SDK data-plane requests such as `Sandbox.files.write()`, the SDK derives a host such as `49983-<sandbox>.cube.app`; absent an explicit CubeProxy address, the Mac tries normal DNS, where this local-only name does not resolve. Thus the observed `fetch failed` occurred before a guest file-transfer request reached CubeProxy. A separate no-VM SDK route probe configured with `proxyNodeIp=127.0.0.1` and `proxyPort=28080` reached the local CubeProxy and returned its expected 404 for an unknown sandbox host. This isolates the diagnostic configuration omission; it does not show a product runtime defect. `src/server/computer.ts::cubeConfig()` already supplies the configured proxy fields to the application SDK.
- Both standalone Chrome diagnostics now default to the local CubeProxy (`127.0.0.1:28080`) when `CUBE_API_URL` points to localhost, while still honoring explicit `CUBE_PROXY_NODE_IP`, `CUBE_PROXY_PORT_HTTP`, `CUBE_PROXY_SCHEME` and `CUBE_SANDBOX_DOMAIN`. Their structured start/failure events include the effective proxy endpoint and sandbox data-plane host; errors include the immediate cause. `docs/local-development.md` now explains the separate API and data-plane tunnels. The earlier launch and cleanup log is `~/.local/share/csa-cube-node/build-logs/chrome-pipe-2026-11-25T01-25-41-431Z-a06b780b-056f-4c55-a318-46e3552d7b5c.jsonl`.
- The one disposable launch authorized for this recovery has been used and cleaned up. No further disposable sandbox was started in this step. Next, run TypeScript/shell syntax checks and a no-VM route probe; if the existing tenant-acceptance workflow needs another sandbox, use its persistent test tenant and record the tenant/run/sandbox correlation instead of silently repeating a disposable launch.

With `dbus-run-session`, Chrome opened a loopback listener after 33 seconds, but `/json/version` returned no body: curl, Python and Node HTTP clients timed out or got an empty reply, and the WebSocket upgrade/`Browser.getVersion` probes also timed out. Thus D-Bus affects startup progress, but it does not fix the unresponsive CDP endpoint. Host logs: `~/.local/share/csa-cube-node/build-logs/beta-chromium-tcp-cdp-20261125-1-detail.log` and `beta-chromium-tcp-dbus-cdp-20261125-1-detail.log`.
- A corrected process resolver now identifies the main binary by `/proc/<pid>/exe` and excludes `--type=` children. On headless TCP run `websocket-20261125034344`, Chrome PID `5906` opened its listener; its main thread was runnable and consumed 2.13 seconds of CPU during a 4.62-second sample (46.1% of VM CPU), while `Chrome_DevTools` waited in `do_epoll_wait`. A later sample on PID `7190` found its main thread runnable with 1.34 seconds of CPU over a 134.9-second guest sampling interval; `Chrome_DevTools` again waited in epoll. This is evidence of a browser-main-thread startup stall, not a confirmed cause.
- Chrome's startup trace produced a 1,224,704-byte Perfetto protobuf. Trace Processor v59.1 parsed it, but the Browser process itself is absent from the recorded process table; child GPU, renderer, tracing and storage processes appear. Chrome logged that Perfetto timed out waiting for processes to acknowledge `BeginTracing`, then reported a Network Service restart. Trace Processor also flagged a `DISCARD` buffer and missing flush period, so this trace cannot identify the Browser main-thread call stack. The JSON trace mode did not create an output file. Host artifact: `~/.local/share/csa-cube-node/build-logs/beta-chrome-startup-trace-20261125-2.json`; capture/inspection logs are in the matching `.log` and `-detail.log` files.
- `scripts/cube/diagnose-chromium-headless-runner.ts` now forwards the requested desktop user and Chrome binary, emits preflight/cleanup failures and retained PTY output on error, records process/thread snapshots, and can export trace bytes without UTF-8 decoding. `scripts/cube/diagnose-chromium-headless.sh` now finds Google Chrome by executable identity and can collect a bounded Chrome startup trace. `tsc --noEmit`, `bash -n scripts/cube/diagnose-chromium-headless.sh`, and `git diff --check` passed before the final trace-format flag adjustment; rerun these checks after the current edits.
- Reconcile this Beta result with the earlier clean-clone runs above: GUI Chrome on candidate `tpl-827a52ee603c40f3992e40dd` completed `Browser.getVersion`, target operations, `Runtime.evaluate` and a screenshot over the DevTools pipe. That success rules out a general claim that this Chrome build or pipe protocol cannot work. It did **not** exercise the product's HTTP/WebSocket pipe bridge end to end. On persistent Beta, the bridge's initial `Browser.getVersion` remained pending; separate headless TCP probes opened a listener but did not return HTTP/CDP responses. The current evidence therefore distinguishes a successful clean-clone pipe session from a Beta-specific startup/transport stall; it does not yet isolate which image, rootfs, profile, session, or runtime difference causes it.
- The product image bundles `chrome-cdp-pipe-bridge.mjs`, which translates loopback HTTP/WebSocket CDP calls into Chrome's pipe protocol. Source inspection confirms it is selected by `csa-desktop-session.sh` when present, but source inspection and a listener alone are not acceptance. Still required: complete the bridge's browser and page WebSocket flow in the tenant desktop, including a Computer Use action and screenshot through the real UI. Until then, keep the tenant desktop marked failed and do not call browser control accepted.
- Current read-only state check at 2026-11-25 12:08 +08:00: Lima `cube-node` is Running; `/data/cubelet` is 80 GiB XFS (about 60 GiB used, 21 GiB available; `df` rounds to 75%). The local API tunnel is 13000; probing 49983 on the Mac was an incorrect port check and must not be used to diagnose a stopped API tunnel. No tunnel restart was needed.
- Still unknown: why this persistent Beta Chrome process does not answer the pipe command, and whether the earlier HTTP stall has the same cause. Do not infer a browser-wide failure, treat the trace warning or missing `strace`/GDB/perf tools as the cause, or repeat long whole-session startup retries as diagnostics. Preserve the Beta tenant, rootfs backups and READY template. Next use one short, controlled browser check on the existing Beta session only after reconciling its current database/Cube state; correlate bridge command IDs and Chrome PID, and capture whether a specific HTTP/WebSocket client command completes. ### 2026-11-25 bound desktop process cleanup after measured delays

- Alpha generation 18 spent 63 seconds between `session.cleanup.start` and `.complete`; Beta generation 19 spent 41 seconds. Source inspection found the session EXIT handler signalled children and then called unbounded `wait` on all background jobs. This is the likely source of the post-failure delay; prior runs did not log per-child wait durations, so no single child is proven responsible.
- `csa-desktop-session.sh` now polls tracked child PIDs and the Chrome process group for up to 6 seconds after SIGTERM, sends SIGKILL if needed, then polls for at most 2 more seconds. It only calls Bash `wait` after the child is no longer live, and logs any process group still present at the deadline. The normal Chrome recovery path uses the same bound and aborts a restart if the previous Chrome group did not stop.
- `bash -n scripts/cube/csa-desktop-session.sh` and `git diff --check` pass. No live desktop restart was run: it would repeat the known long Chrome readiness path and would not explain the underlying Chrome/CDP failure. Live cleanup timing remains to be verified on a future authorized tenant start.

### 2026-11-25 same-UID ptrace comparison and Chrome process inspection

- Reused idle Beta sandbox `315bbf88bd0e497a92099d23845e7a55`, generation 19, with zero active Agent runs. No VM was created, no package was installed, and no DSH task was submitted. A SHA-256-verified cached ARM64 `strace` package was staged only under `/tmp` and removed by the runner.
- As UID 1000, strace successfully followed `/usr/bin/true`; a separate UID-1000 tracer also attached to an unrelated UID-1000 `sleep` process (`strace: Process 13829 attached`) and observed its exit. This establishes that same-UID PTRACE_SEIZE is available for an ordinary process in this guest.
- During no-CDP Chrome launch, the browser leader was UID/GID 1000, `TracerPid=0`, `NoNewPrivs=0`, `Seccomp=0`, and in the same user, PID and mount namespaces as the diagnostic shell. `/proc/<chrome-pid>/mem` was owned by UID 1000 and could be opened read-only by UID 1000. Nevertheless, same-UID strace attachment to Chrome returned `ptrace(PTRACE_SEIZE, 14367): Operation not permitted`. The process identity rules out a different user namespace and guest seccomp as explanations; the exact Chrome-specific/kernel/LSM reason remains unknown. A successful `/proc/<pid>/mem` open does not settle this: Linux checks it with `PTRACE_MODE_ATTACH_FSCREDS`, while `PTRACE_SEIZE` checks `PTRACE_MODE_ATTACH_REALCREDS`; see the [procfs implementation](https://github.com/torvalds/linux/blob/master/fs/proc/base.c) and [ptrace implementation](https://github.com/torvalds/linux/blob/master/kernel/ptrace.c). Do not infer dumpability from the procfs result alone.
- The no-CDP `--dump-dom about:blank` probe again produced no HTML and exited on its bounded timeout. The runner verified no Chrome processes remained and removed the unique profile and HOME. No tenant files or browser profile were changed; guest overlay remained around 7.4 GiB free.
- Host logs: `~/.local/share/csa-cube-node/build-logs/guest-ptrace-selftest-20261125-attach-short.log`, `~/.local/share/csa-cube-node/build-logs/chrome-same-uid-strace-20261125.log`, `~/.local/share/csa-cube-node/build-logs/chrome-same-uid-strace-extended-20261125.log`, and `~/.local/share/csa-cube-node/build-logs/chrome-proc-mem-identity-20261125.log`.
- These traces narrow the blocker to Chrome's process-specific attachment behavior while confirming that the browser cannot render even without CDP. They still do not identify the code path making Chrome stall. The task is blocked before DSH/DeepSeek startup. Next investigation must use a method that does not repeat the same PTRACE_SEIZE attempt; keep the current VM and avoid rebuilding templates until that probe identifies a concrete image/runtime change.

### 2026-11-25 microVM Chrome vs Docker control

- Reused idle Beta sandbox `315bbf88bd0e497a92099d23845e7a55` (generation 19, zero active runs) and existing local image `coke-super-agent-desktop:local`; no VM/template was created, no package installed, no tenant browser profile touched and no DSH task submitted.
- In the Cube microVM, Chrome 158.0.8127.88 with `--headless=new --disable-gpu --disable-dev-shm-usage --dump-dom data:text/html,CSA_RENDER_PROBE` did not produce HTML before the 22-second bound. Repeating with Chrome's sandbox enabled (omitting `--no-sandbox`) also failed to render. A `/proc` snapshot showed the browser leader accumulating CPU ticks while the command remained stuck; stderr had no fatal startup error.
- In a local Docker container from the same image, the same Chrome binary, URL, D-Bus session and browser user rendered `<body>CSA_RENDER_PROBE</body>` and exited 0 in about one second. The first local Docker attempt was invalid because the probe created Crashpad directories as root; its log reports a permission/path error. After recreating those directories as UID 1000, both the uncapped control and a control capped at 2 CPUs/2 GiB rendered successfully. Do not count the first harness failure as product evidence. The capped run shows those limits alone are insufficient to reproduce the Cube failure, but the Docker Linux kernel/VM and scheduler are not identical to the Cube path.
- Read-only host topology: Cube node `cube-node` is Lima 2.3.0 `vmType=vz`, ARM64, 4 vCPU/8 GiB; its Ubuntu 24.04 guest runs a 6.8 ARM64 kernel and exposes `/dev/kvm`, where Cube starts each microVM. Docker Desktop's LinuxKit VM is separate. Thus this test path nests a Cube microVM inside Lima/VZ, while the Docker control runs directly as a container in Docker Desktop's VM. Nested virtualization is a plausible performance amplifier, not a proven explanation for Chrome's hang. At measurement time the Mac had 16 GiB physical RAM and reported 4.69/5 GiB swap used with 35% system-wide free-memory estimate; host resource contention may compound latency, but there is no clean-load baseline proving causality.
- This is consistent with the CubeSandbox project's own note that Apple Silicon local development adds a Linux host VM before the sandbox VM and makes lifecycle benchmarks less representative ([upstream issue #975](https://github.com/TencentCloud/CubeSandbox/issues/975)); it is not proof that nested virtualization caused this specific Chrome failure.
- Host logs: `~/.local/share/csa-cube-node/build-logs/chrome-data-url-micovm-sandbox-20261125.log`, `chrome-data-url-local-docker-20261125.log` (initial invalid root-owned probe), `chrome-data-url-local-docker-20261125-retry.log`, and `chrome-data-url-local-docker-2cpu-2g-20261125.log`.
- Next step: stop repeating browser/profile/ptrace variants and do not rebuild the image. Capture one aligned, short CPU/memory/scheduler sample across Mac, Lima node and the existing microVM during a single render; if it cannot distinguish the nested path from host contention, use a native ARM64 KVM host as a baseline before deciding whether to retain Mac as the data plane.

### 2026-11-25 bounded no-CDP Chrome CPU sample and profiler setup failures

- Reused idle Beta sandbox `315bbf88bd0e497a92099d23845e7a55`, template `tpl-d92cea70efc040cfb32580f2`, generation 19, with zero queued/working Agent runs. The probe used an isolated `/tmp` profile and a local `data:text/html,CSA_RENDER_PROBE` URL. It did not create a VM, change a template, install a guest system package, submit a DSH task or touch a tenant browser profile.
- Headless Chrome remained alive until the 35-second deadline and returned zero HTML bytes; exit was 137 after the timeout path. At the end, cleanup found no matching Chrome process. The guest cgroup was limited to 2 vCPU and 2 GiB; memory was about 316 to 326 MiB, memory events showed no OOM, and CPU/memory/I/O PSI stayed at zero. Across the measured 20.401-second interval, the browser leader used 406 ticks (about 4.06 CPU seconds); sampled ThreadPoolForegroundWorker threads together used 429 ticks (about 4.29 CPU seconds). The Chrome process grew from 26 to 43 threads. This confirms sustained user-space CPU work with no guest memory pressure; it does not identify which Chrome component is responsible or rule out all host scheduling effects.
- A same-UID `strace -f -p` attempt targeted the Chrome leader and its 35 threads for four seconds, but ended with timeout 124 and zero trace/stderr bytes. Because attach completion was not confirmed, this is inconclusive about Chrome's syscalls and must not be described as proof that Chrome made no syscalls. Chrome stderr contained only GPU-device/sandbox and enterprise-policy messages in the captured tail; none establishes a fatal cause.
- The cached Debian Bookworm perf rootfs was SHA-256-valid and ARM64, but its `libstdc++.so.6` required GLIBC 2.36 while this Ubuntu 22.04 guest has GLIBC 2.35. The official Ubuntu Jammy ARM64 perf package avoided that GLIBC floor but required five libraries absent from the minimal desktop rootfs (`libdw`, `libslang`, `libpython3.10`, `libnuma`, `libtraceevent`). A temporary compatibility attempt using the cached perf libraries still did not pass its `perf --version` check within eight seconds. The Ubuntu Jammy GDB rootfs extracted, but its `gdb --version` check also exceeded the ten-second bound. These are profiler-tool setup failures, not evidence about Chrome's cause. No package was installed into the guest; staging was confined to `/tmp` and cleanup traps ran.
- Confirmed state: application and Cube API health are good; the existing Beta VM is running with desktop state failed; no active Agent run exists. The current failure is before DSH startup. The exact Chrome startup code path remains unknown. The investigation took longer because bounded Chrome launches each cost tens of seconds and the attempted symbol/ptrace tools did not produce a usable stack; I also spent time on a perf bundle incompatible with the guest's GLIBC.
- Next step: verify a stack-capable diagnostic against the guest's Ubuntu 22.04 libraries and launch it successfully before repeating Chrome. Do not repeat the same broad `strace` attachment or treat nested virtualization, GPU warnings, resource limits or DeepSeek as a proven root cause.

### 2026-11-25 bounded Chrome render with Mac and Lima resource sampling

- Reused idle Beta sandbox `315bbf88bd0e497a92099d23845e7a55`, generation 19; the database preflight showed zero active Agent runs. No VM/template was created, no package was installed, and no tenant browser profile was touched. A bounded 25-second headless `data:text/html,CSA_RENDER_PROBE` run returned no HTML and timed out; its wrapper exited 7 after Chrome exited 137. Guest cleanup completed, and a follow-up read-only inspection found no matching Chrome process, no staged strace package or diagnostic script, and 7.4 GiB free on the overlay.
- At the one captured guest snapshot, Chrome had 28 threads; its browser leader was sleeping in syscall 98 (futex on arm64). One `ThreadPoolForegroundWorker` had accumulated 1,251 CPU ticks since Chrome started, and the leader had 301 ticks. These are single-snapshot cumulative counts, not a sampled rate. Guest CPU, memory and I/O PSI were zero; cgroup memory was 314,327,040 bytes of a 2 GiB limit. The sample does not identify the worker's code path or explain why rendering did not finish.
- During the run, Mac `top` snapshots reported roughly 40–60% total CPU idle, about 6.9–7.1 GiB in the compressor, and 5.1 GiB of 6 GiB swap used; system-wide free-memory estimate was 33–34%. The Cube Lima guest had about 3.88 GiB `MemAvailable` and no memory PSI. Its CPU `some avg10` PSI rose from 2.14% in the minute before the run to 15–17% in samples during the render, while CPU `full` stayed zero. This is evidence of increased CPU scheduling delay inside the Lima host during the diagnostic, not proof that the Chrome hang was caused by nested virtualization or Mac-wide CPU saturation. A host process listing immediately before the run identified PID 79224 as the Cube `cube-node` Virtualization process by its open disk files; its preceding one-second sample showed 43.6% CPU. The concurrent sampler accidentally filtered out that PID's rows, so its exact CPU usage during the render was not captured.
- The diagnostic itself also showed a sampling defect: process identity collection delayed the first thread snapshot, and the second snapshot occurred after the 25-second Chrome deadline. Therefore no per-thread CPU delta was produced. Do not repeat this exact sampling setup. Before another Chrome launch, fix the sampler to record thread snapshots promptly and continuously sample the specific `cube-node` Virtualization PID; then correlate host, Lima and guest measurements in the same bounded run.
